P0418 ELEMENTARY THEORY OF MAGNETISM.
P0419 371.] CERTAIN bodies, as, for instance, the iron ore called load stone, the earth itself, and pieces of steel which have been sub jected to certain treatment, are found to possess the following properties, and are called Magnets.
P0420 If, near any part of the earth s surface except the Magnetic Poles, a magnet be suspended so as to turn freely about a vertical axis, it will in general tend to set itself in a certain azimuth, and
P0421 if disturbed from this position it will oscillate about if. An un
P0422 magnetized body has no such tendency, but is in equilibrium in all azimuths alike.
P0423 372.] It is found that the force which acts on the body tends to cause a certain line in the body, called the Axis of the Magnet, to become parallel to a certain line in space, called the Direction
P0424 of the Magnetic Force.
P0425 Let us suppose the magnet suspended so as to be free to turn
P0426 in all directions about a fixed point. To eliminate the action of
P0427 its weight we may suppose this point to be its centre of gravity. Let it come to a position^of equilibrium. Mark two points on
P0428 the magnet, and note their positions in space. Then let the
P0429 magnet be placed in a new position of equilibrium, and note the
P0430 positions in space of the two marked points on the magnet.
P0431 Since the axis of the magnet coincides with the direction of
P0432 magnetic force in both positions, we have to find that line in
P0433 the magnet which occupies the same position in space before and
P0434 VOL. II. B 2 ELEMENTARY THEORY OF MAGNETISM. [373
P0435 after the motion. It appears, from the theory of the motion of
P0436 &amp;gt;;{ ^ bodies of invariable form, that such a line always exists, and that a motion equivalent to the actual motion might have taken place
P0437 by simple rotation round this line. To find the line, join the first and last positions of each of the
P0438 marked points, and draw planes bisecting these lines at right
P0439 angles. The intersection of these planes will be the line required, which indicates the direction of the axis of the magnet and the
P0440 direction of the magnetic force in space.
P0441 The method just described is not convenient for the practical
P0442 determination of these directions. We shall return to this subject
P0443 when we treat of Magnetic Measurements.
P0444 The direction of the magnetic force is found to be different at
P0445 different parts of the earth s surface. If the end of the axis of
P0446 the magnet which points in a northerly direction be marked, it has been found that the direction in which it sets itself in general
P0447 deviates from the true meridian to a considerable extent, and that
P0448 the marked end points on the whole downwards in the northern
P0449 fc hemisphere and upwards in the southern.
P0450 The azimuth of the direction of the magnetic force, measured
P0451 from the true north in a westerly direction, is called the Variation,
P0452 or the Magnetic Declination. The angle between the direction of
P0453 the magnetic force and the horizontal plane is called the Magnetic
P0454 Dip. These two angles determine the direction of the magnetic force, and, when the magnetic intensity is also known, the magnetic force is completely determined. The determination of the values
P0455 of these three elements at different parts of the earth s surface,
P0456 the discussion of the manner in which they vary according to the
P0457 place and time of observation, and the investigation of the causes
P0458 of the magnetic force and its variations, constitute the science of
P0459 Terrestrial Magnetism.
P0460 373.] Let us now suppose that the axes of several magnets have
P0461 been determined, and the end of each which points north marked.
P0462 Then, if one of these be freely suspended and another brought
P0463 near it, it is found that two marked ends repel each other, that
P0464 a marked and an unmarked end attract each other, and that two
P0465 unmarked ends repel each other.
P0466 If the magnets are in the form of long rods or wires, uniformly and longitudinally magnetized, see below, Art. 384, it is found
P0467 that the greatest manifestation of force occurs when the end of
P0468 one magnet is held near the end of the other, and that the 374-] LAW OF MAGNETIC FORCE. 3
P0469 phenomena can be accounted for by supposing- that like ends of the magnets repel each other, that unlike ends attract each other, and that the intermediate parts of the magnets have no sensible mutual action.
P0470 The ends of a long thin magnet are commonly called its Poles. In the case of an indefinitely thin magnet, uniformly magnetized throughout its length, the extremities act as centres of force, and the rest of the magnet appears devoid of magnetic action. In all actual magnets the magnetization deviates from uniformity, so that no single points can be taken as the poles. Coulomb, how ever, by using long thin rods magnetized with care, succeeded in establishing the law of force between two magnetic poles *.
P0471 The repulsion between two magnetic poles is in the straight line joining them, and is numerically equal to the product of the strengths of the poles divided by the square of the distance between them.
P0472 374.] This law, of course, assumes that the strength of each
P0473 pole is measured in terms of a certain unit, the magnitude of which
P0474 may be deduced from the terms of the law.
P0475 The unit-pole is a pole which points north, and is such that,
P0476 when placed at unit distance from another unit-pole, it repels it
P0477 with unit offeree, the unit of force being defined as in Art. 6. A
P0478 pole which points south is reckoned negative.

P0491 Balance, and confirmed by the experiments of Gauss and Weber, and of all observers in magnetic observatories, who are every day making measurements of magnetic quantities, and who obtain results which would be inconsistent with each other if the law of force

P0493 376.] The quantity which we have hitherto called the strength of a pole may also be called a quantity of Magnetism, provided we attribute no properties to Magnetism except those observed
P0494 in the poles of magnets. Since the expression of the law of force between given quantities
P0495 of Magnetism has exactly the same mathematical form as the law of force between quantities of Electricity of equal numerical value, much of the mathematical treatment of magnetism must be similar to that of electricity. There are, however, other properties
P0496 of magnets which must be borne in mind, and which may throw
P0497 some light on the electrical properties of bodies.

P0499 377.] The quantity of magnetism at one pole of a magnet is always equal and opposite to that at the other, or more generally
P0500 In every Magnet the total quantity of Magnetism (reckoned alge
P0501 Hence in a field of force which is uniform and parallel throughout the space occupied by the magnet, the force acting on the marked end of the magnet is exactly equal, opposite and parallel to that on the unmarked end, so that the resultant of the forces is a statical couple, tending to place the axis of the magnet in a determinate direction, but not to move the magnet as a whole in any direction.
P0502 This may be easily proved by putting the magnet into a small vessel and floating it in water. The vessel will turn in a certain direction, so as to bring the axis of the magnet as near as possible
P0503 to the direction of the earth s magnetic force, but there will be no motion of the vessel as a whole in any direction ; so that there can be no excess of the force towards the north over that towards the
P0504 south, or the reverse. It may also be shewn from the fact that
P0505 magnetizing a piece of steel does not alter its weight. It does alter the apparent position of its centre of gravity, causing it in these 380.] MAGNETIC MATTER/ 5
P0506 latitudes to shift along the axis towards the north. The centre of inertia, as determined by the phenomena of rotation, remains
P0507 378.] If the middle of a long thin magnet be examined, it is found to possess no magnetic properties, but if the magnet be broken at that point, each of the pieces is found to have a magnetic pole at the place of fracture, and this new pole is exactly equal and opposite to the other pole belonging to that piece. It is impossible, either by magnetization, or by breaking magnets, or by any other means, to procure a magnet whose poles are un
P0508 If we break the long thin magnet into a number of short pieces we shall obtain a series of short magnets, each of which has poles of nearly the same strength as those of the original long magnet.
P0509 This multiplication of poles is not necessarily a creation of energy,
P0510 for we must remember that after breaking the magnet we have to do work to separate the parts, in consequence of their attraction
P0511 379.] Let us now put all the pieces of the magnet together as at first. At each point of junction there will be two poles
P0512 exactly equal and of opposite kinds, placed in contact, so that their united action on any other pole will be null. The magnet, thus rebuilt, has therefore the same properties as at first, namely two poles, one at each end, equal and opposite to each other, and the part between these poles exhibits no magnetic action.
P0513 Since, in this case, we know the long magnet to be made up of little short magnets, and since the phenomena are the same as in the case of the unbroken magnet, we may regard the magnet, even before being broken, as made up of small particles, each of which has two equal and opposite poles. If we suppose all magnets to be made up of such particles, it is evident that since the algebraical quantity of magnetism in each particle is zero, the quantity in the whole magnet will also be zero, or in other words,
P0514 its poles will be of equal strength but of opposite kind.
P0515 Theory of Magnetic Matter?
P0516 380.] Since the form of the law of magnetic action is identical with that of electric action, the same reasons which can be given for attributing electric phenomena to the action of one flu id or two fluids can also be used in favour of the existence of a
P0517 magnetic matter, or of two kinds of magnetic matter, fluid or 6 ELEMENTARY THEORY OF MAGNETISM. [380.
P0518 otherwise. In fact, a theory of magnetic matter, if used in a purely mathematical sense, cannot fail to explain the phenomena,
P0519 provided new laws are freely introduced to account for the actual
P0520 One of these new laws must be that the magnetic fluids cannot pass from one molecule or particle of the magnet to another, but
P0521 that the process of magnetization consists in separating to a certain extent the two fluids within each particle, and causing the one fluid to be more concentrated at one end, and the other fluid to be more concentrated at the other end of the particle. This is the theory of Poisson.
P0522 A particle of a magnetizable body is, on this theory, analogous
P0523 to a small insulated conductor without charge, which on the two fluid theory contains indefinitely large but exactly equal quantities
P0524 of the two electricities. When an electromotive force acts on the
P0525 conductor, it separates the electricities, causing them to become manifest at opposite sides of the conductor. In a similar manner, according to this theory, the magnetizing force causes the two kinds of magnetism, which were originally in a neutralized state, to be separated, and to appear at opposite sides of the magnetized
P0526 In certain substances, such as soft iron and those magnetic substances which cannot be permanently magnetized, this magnetic condition, like the electrification of the conductor, disappears when the inducing force is removed. In other substances, such as hard steel, the magnetic condition is produced with difficulty, and, when produced, remains after the removal of the inducing force.
P0527 This is expressed by saying that in the latter case there is a Coercive Force, tending to prevent alteration in the magnetization, which must be overcome before the power of a magnet can be either increased or diminished. In the case of the electrified body this would correspond to a kind of electric resistance, which, unlike the resistance observed in metals, would be equivalent to complete insulation for electromotive forces below a certain value.
P0528 This theory of magnetism, like the corresponding theory of electricity, is evidently too large for the facts, and requires to be restricted by artificial conditions. For it not only gives no reason
P0529 why one body may not differ from another on account of having
P0530 more of both fluids, but it enables us to say what would be the properties of a body containing an excess of one magnetic fluid.
P0531 It is true that a reason is given why such a body cannot exist, 381.] MAGNETIC POLARIZATION. 7
P0532 but this reason is only introduced as an after-thought to explain this particular fact. It does not grow out of the theory.
P0533 381.] We must therefore seek for a mode of expression which
P0534 shall not be capable of expressing too much, and which shall leave
P0535 room for the introduction of new ideas as these are developed from new facts. This, I think, we shall obtain if we begin by saying
P0536 that the particles of a magnet are Polarized.
P0537 Meaning of the term Polarization?
P0538 When a particle of a body possesses properties related to a
P0539 certain line or direction in the body, and when the body, retaining
P0540 these properties, is turned so that this direction is reversed, then
P0541 if as regards other bodies these properties of the particle are
P0542 reversed, the particle, in reference to these properties, is said to be polarized, and the properties are said to constitute a particular
P0543 kind of polarization.
P0544 Thus we may say that the rotation of a body about an axis
P0545 constitutes a kind of polarization, because if, while the rotation
P0546 continues, the direction of the axis is turned end for end, the body will be rotating in the opposite direction as regards space.
P0547 A conducting particle through which there is a current of elec
P0548 tricity may be said to be polarized, because if it were turned round,
P0549 and if the current continued to flow in the same direction as regards the particle, its direction in space would be reversed.
P0550 In short, if any mathematical or physical quantity is of the
P0551 nature of a vector, as defined in Art. 11, then any body or particle
P0552 to which this directed quantity or vector belongs may be said to
P0553 be Polarized *9 because it has opposite properties in the two opposite
P0554 directions or poles of the directed quantity.
P0555 The poles of the earth, for example, have reference to its rotation, and have accordingly different names.
P0556 * The word Polarization has been used in a sense not consistent with this in Optics, where a ray of light is said to be polarized when it has properties relating to its sides, which are identical on opposite sides of the ray. This kind of polarization refers to another kind of Directed Quantity, which may be called a Dipolar Quantity, in opposition to the former kind, which may be called Unipolar.
P0557 When a dipolar quantity is turned end for end it remains the same as before.
P0558 Tensions and Pressures in solid bodies, Extensions, Compressions and Distortions and most of the optical, electrical, and magnetic properties of crystallized bodies are dipolar quantities.
P0559 The property produced by magnetism in transparent bodies of twisting the plane of polarization of the incident light, is, like magnetism itself, a unipolar property. The rotatory property referred to in Art. 303 is also unipolar. 8 ELEMENTARY THEORY OF MAGNETISM. [382.
P0560 Meaning of the term Magnetic Polarization.
P0561 382.] In speaking of the state of the particles of a magnet as magnetic polarization, we imply that each of the smallest parts
P0562 into which a magnet may be divided has certain properties related
P0563 to a definite direction through the particle, called its Axis of Magnetization, and that the properties related to one end of this axis are opposite to the properties related to the other end.
P0564 The properties which we attribute to the particle are of the same kind as those which we observe in the complete magnet, and in assuming that the particles possess these properties, we only assert what we can prove by breaking the magnet up into small pieces,
P0565 for each of these is found to be a magnet.

P0807 This investigation may be compared with that by which the
P0808 potential of a system of gravitating matter is expanded. In the latter case, the most convenient point to assume as the origin is the centre of gravity of the system, and the most convenient axes are the principal axes of inertia through that point.
P0809 In the case of the magnet, the point corresponding to the centre of gravity is at an infinite distance in the direction of the axis, 394 ] CONVENTION RESPECTING SIGNS. 19
P0810 and the point which we call the centre of the magnet is a point
P0811 having- different properties from those of the centre of gravity.
P0812 The quantities If, M, N correspond to the moments of inertia,
P0813 M N and P, Q, R to the products of inertia of a material body, except
P0814 that Z, and are not necessarily positive quantities.

P0816 spherical harmonic of the second order is of the sectorial form, having its axis coinciding with that of the magnet, and this is true of no other point.
P0817 When the magnet is symmetrical on all sides of this axis, as
P0818 in the case of a figure of revolution, the term involving the harmonic
P0819 of the second order disappears entirely.
P0820 393.] At all parts of the earth s surface, except some parts of the Polar regions, one end of a magnet points towards the north,
P0821 or at least in a northerly direction, and the other in a southerly direction. In speaking of the ends of a magnet we shall adopt the popular method of calling the end which points to the north the
P0822 north end of the magnet. When, however, we speak in the language of the theory of magnetic fluids we shall use the words Boreal and Austral. Boreal magnetism is an imaginary kind of matter supposed to be most abundant in the northern, parts of the earth, and Austral magnetism is the imaginary magnetic matter which prevails in the southern regions of the earth. The magnetism of the north end of a magnet is Austral, and that of
P0823 the south end is Boreal. When therefore we speak of the north
P0824 and south ends of a magnet we do not compare the magnet with
P0825 the earth as the great magnet, but merely express the position
P0826 which the magnet endeavours to take up when free to move. When, on the other hand, we wish to compare the distribution of ima ginary magnetic fluid in the magnet with that in the earth we shall use the more grandiloquent words Boreal and Austral magnetism.
P0827 394.] In speaking of a field of magnetic force we shall use the phrase Magnetic North to indicate the direction in which the north end of a compass needle would point if placed in the field
P0828 In speaking of a line of magnetic force we shall always suppose it to be traced from magnetic south to magnetic north, and shall call this direction positive. In the same way the direction of magnetization of a magnet is indicated by a line drawn from the south end of the magnet towards the north end, and the end of the magnet which points north is reckoned the positive end. 20 ELEMENTARY THEORY OF MAGNETISM. \_394-

P0830 that end of a magnet which points north, as positive. If we denote its numerical value by m&amp;gt; then the magnetic potential

P0834 395.] WE have already (Art. 386) determined the magnetic
P0835 potential at a given point due to a magnet, the magnetization of which is given at every point of its substance, and we have shewn

P0838 The magnetic potential, as thus denned, is found by the same mathematical process, whether the given point is outside the magnet or within it. The force exerted on a unit magnetic pole placed at any point outside the magnet is deduced from the potential by the same process of differentiation as in the corresponding electrical

P0841 To determine by experiment the magnetic force at a point within the magnet we must begin by removing part of the magnetized substance, so as to form a cavity within which we are to place the magnetic pole. The force acting on the pole will depend, in general, in the form of this cavity, and on the inclination of the walls of the cavity to the direction of magnetization. Hence it is necessary, in order to avoid ambiguity in speaking of the magnetic force within a magnet, to specify the form and position of the cavity within which the force is to be measured. It is manifest that
P0842 when the form and position of the cavity is specified, the point within it at which the magnetic pole is placed must be regarded as 22 MAGNETIC FORCE AND MAGNETIC INDUCTION. [396.
P0843 no longer within the substance of the magnet, and therefore the ordinary methods of determining the force become at once applicable.

P0961 netic sphondyloids is mathematically true, if understood of the

P0963 The magnetic force and the magnetic induction are identical
P0964 outside the magnet, but within the substance of the magnet they
P0965 must be carefully distinguished. In a straight uniformly mag
P0966 netized bar the magnetic force due to the magnet itself is from the end which points north, which we call the positive pole, towards
P0967 the south end or negative pole, both within the magnet and in
P0968 The magnetic induction, on the other hand, is from the positive
P0969 pole to the negative outside the magnet, and from the negative
P0970 pole to the positive within the magnet, so that the lines and tubes
P0971 of induction are re-entering or cyclic figures.
P0972 The importance of the magnetic induction as a physical quantity will be more clearly seen when we study electromagnetic phe
P0973 nomena. When the magnetic field is explored by a moving wire,
P0974 as in Faraday s Exp. Res. 3076, it is the magnetic induction and
P0975 not the magnetic force which is directly measured.

P1184 Definition. The solid angle subtended at a given point by a closed curve is measured by the area of a spherical surface whose centre is the given point and whose radius is unity, the outline of which is traced by the intersection of the radius vector with the sphere as it traces the closed curve. This area is to be reckoned positive or negative according as it lies on the left or the right hand of the path of the radius vector as seen from the given point.

P1336 INDUCED MAGNETIZATION.
P1337 424.] WE have hitherto considered the actual distribution of
P1338 magnetization in a magnet as given explicitly among the data
P1339 of the investigation. We have not made any assumption as to
P1340 whether this magnetization is permanent or temporary, except in those parts of our reasoning in which we have supposed the magnet broken up into small portions, or small portions removed from the magnet in such a way as not to alter the magnetization of any part.
P1341 We have now to consider the magnetization of bodies with
P1342 respect to the mode in which it may be produced and changed.
P1343 A bar of iron held parallel to the direction of the earth s magnetic
P1344 force is found to become magnetic, with its poles turned the op posite way from those of the earth, or the same way as those of a compass needle in stable equilibrium.
P1345 Any piece of soft iron placed in a magnetic field is found to exhibit
P1346 magnetic properties. If it be placed in a part of the field where the magnetic force is great, as between the poles of a horse-shoe magnet, the magnetism of the iron becomes intense. If the iron is removed from the magnetic field, its magnetic properties are greatly weakened or disappear entirely. If the magnetic properties of the iron depend entirely on the magnetic force of the field in which it is placed, and vanish when it is removed from the field, it is called Soft iron. Iron which is soft in the magnetic sense is also soft in the literal sense. It is easy to bend it and give it a permanent set, and difficult to break it.
P1347 Iron which retains its magnetic properties when removed from the magnetic field is called Hard iron. Such iron does not take 425.] SOFT AND HARD STEEL. 45
P1348 up the magnetic state so readily as soft iron. The operation of hammering-, or any other kind of vibration, allows hard iron under
P1349 the influence of magnetic force to assume the magnetic state more readily, and to part with it more readily when the magnetizing force is removed. Iron which is magnetically hard is also more stiff to bend and more apt to break.
P1350 The processes of hammering, rolling, wire-drawing, and sudden cooling tend to harden iron, and that of annealing tends to
P1351 The magnetic as well as the mechanical differences between steel of hard and soft temper are much greater than those between hard and soft iron. Soft steel is almost as easily magnetized and de magnetized as iron, while the hardest steel is the best material for magnets which we wish to be permanent.
P1352 Cast iron, though it contains more carbon than steel, is not
P1353 so retentive of magnetization.
P1354 If a magnet could be constructed so that the distribution of its magnetization is not altered by any magnetic force brought to act upon it, it might be called a rigidly magnetized body. The only known body which fulfils this condition is a conducting circuit round which a constant electric current is made to flow.
P1355 Such a circuit exhibits magnetic properties, and may therefore be
P1356 called an electromagnet, but these magnetic properties are not
P1357 affected by the other magnetic forces in the field. We shall return
P1358 to this subject in Part IV.
P1359 All actual magnets, whether made of hardened steel or of load stone, are found to be affected by any magnetic force which is brought to bear upon them.
P1360 It is convenient, for scientific purposes, to make a distinction between the permanent and the temporary magnetization, defining the permanent magnetization as that which exists independently of the magnetic force, and the temporary magnetization as that
P1361 which depends on this force. We must observe, however, that
P1362 this distinction is not founded on a knowledge of the intimate
P1363 nature of magnetizable substances : it is only the expression of an hypothesis introduced for the sake of bringing calculation to
P1364 bear on the phenomena. We shall return to the physical theory
P1365 of magnetization in Chapter VI.
P1366 425.] At present we shall investigate the temporary magnet ization on the assumption that the magnetization of any particle
P1367 of the substance depends solely on the magnetic force acting on 46 INDUCED MAGNETIZATION. [425.
P1368 that particle. This magnetic force may arise partly from external
P1369 causes, and partly from the temporary magnetization of neigh
P1370 A body thus magnetized in virtue of the action of magnetic
P1371 force, is said to be magnetized by induction, and the magnetization is said to be induced by the magnetizing force.
P1372 The magnetization induced by a given magnetizing force differs in different substances. It is greatest in the purest and softest iron, in which the ratio of the magnetization to the magnetic force may reach the value 32, or even 45 *.
P1373 Other substances, such as the metals nickel and cobalt, are capable of an inferior degree of magnetization, and all substances when subjected to a sufficiently strong magnetic force, are found
P1374 to give indications of polarity.
P1375 When the magnetization is in the same direction as the magnetic
P1376 force, as in iron, nickel, cobalt, &c., the substance is called Para
P1377 magnetic, Ferromagnetic, or more simply Magnetic. When the
P1378 induced magnetization is in the direction opposite to the magnetic force, as in bismuth, &c., the substance is said to be Diamagnetic.
P1379 In all these substances the ratio of the magnetization to the magnetic force which produces it is exceedingly small, being only
P1380 about 4 o (H) o Q m the case f bismuth, which is the most highly
P1381 diamagnetic substance known. In crystallized, strained, and organized substances the direction
P1382 of the magnetization does not always coincide with that of the magnetic force which produces it. The relation between the com ponents of magnetization, referred to axes fixed in the body, and
P1383 those of the magnetic force, may be expressed by a system of three linear equations. Of the nine coefficients involved in these equa tions we shall shew that only six are independent. The phenomena of bodies of this kind are classed under the name of Magnecrystallic
P1384 When placed in a field of magnetic force, crystals tend to set
P1385 themselves so that the axis of greatest paramagnetic, or of least diamagnetic, induction is parallel to the lines of magnetic force. See Art. 435.
P1386 In soft iron, the direction of the magnetization coincides with that of the magnetic force at the point, and for small values of the magnetic force the magnetization is nearly proportional to it.
P1387 * Thaten, Nova Ada, Reg. Soc. Sc., Upsal., 1863. 427.] PROBLEM OF INDUCED MAGNETIZATION. 47
P1388 As the magnetic force increases, however, the magnetization in creases more slowly, and it would appear from experiments described
P1389 in Chap. VI, that there is a limiting value of the magnetization, beyond which it cannot pass, whatever be the value of the
P1390 magnetic force. In the following outline of the theory of induced magnetism,
P1391 we shall begin by supposing the magnetization proportional to the magnetic force, and in the same line with it.

P1532 430.] The mathematical theory of magnetic induction was first given by Poisson *. The physical hypothesis on which he founded his theory was that of two magnetic fluids, an hypothesis which has the same mathematical advantages and physical difficulties as the theory of two electric fluids. In order, however, to explain the fact that, though a piece of soft iron can be magnetized by induction, it cannot be charged with unequal quantities of the two kinds of magnetism, he supposes that the substance in general is a non-conductor of these fluids, and that only certain small portions of the substance contain the fluids under circumstances in which they are free to obey the forces which act on them. These small magnetic elements of the substance contain each pre cisely equal quantities of the two fluids, and within each element the fluids move with perfect freedom, but the fluids can never pass from one magnetic element to another.
P1533 The problem therefore is of the same kind as that relating to a number of small conductors of electricity disseminated through a dielectric insulating medium. The conductors may be of any form provided they are small and do not touch each other.
P1534 If they are elongated bodies all turned in the same general
P1535 * Memoires de I lnstitut, 1824. 54 INDUCED MAGNETIZATION. [43O.
P1536 direction, or if they are crowded more in one direction than another, the medium, as Poisson himself shews, will not be isotropic. Poisson therefore, to avoid useless intricacy, examines the case in which each magnetic element is spherical, and the elements are dissem
P1537 inated without regard to axes. He supposes that the whole volume
P1538 of all the magnetic elements in unit of volume of the substance

P1627 434.] The corresponding solution in two dimensions is graphically represented in Fig. XV, at the end of this volume. The lines of induction, which at a distance from the centre of the figure are nearly horizontal, are represented as disturbed by a cylindric rod magnetized transversely and placed in its position of stable equi librium. The lines which cut this system at right angles represent the equipotential surfaces, one of which is a cylinder. The large dotted circle represents the section of a cylinder of a paramagnetic substance, and the dotted horizontal straight lines within it, which are continuous with the external lines of induction, represent the lines of induction within the substance. The dotted vertical lines
P1628 represent the internal equipotential surfaces, and are continuous with the external system. It will be observed that the lines of induction are drawn nearer together within the substance, and the equipotential surfaces are separated farther apart by the paramag netic cylinder, which, in the language of Faraday, conducts the lines of induction better than the surrounding medium.

P1848 441.] Almost every part of magnetic science finds its use in navigation. The directive action of the earth s magnetism on the compass needle is the only method of ascertaining the ship s course when the sun and stars are hid. The declination of the needle from
P1849 the true meridian seemed at first to be a hindrance to the appli cation of the compass to navigation, but after this difficulty had been overcome by the construction of magnetic charts it appeared likely that the declination itsylf would assist the mariner in de
P1850 termining his ship s place. The greatest difficulty in navigation had always been to ascertain
P1851 the longitude ; but since the declination is different at different points on the same parallel of latitude, an observation of the de clination together with a knowledge of the latitude would enable the mariner to find his position on the magnetic chart.
P1852 But in recent times iron is so largely used in the construction of ships that it has become impossible to use the compass at all without taking into account the action of the ship, as a magnetic body, on the needle.
P1853 To determine the distribution of magnetism in a mass of iron of any form under the influence of the earth s magnetic force, even though not subjected to mechanical strain or other disturb ances, is, as we have seen, a very difficult problem.
P1854 In this case, however, the problem is simplified by the following
P1855 The compass is supposed to be placed with its centre at a fixed point of the ship, and so far from any iron that the magnetism of the needle does not induce any perceptible magnetism in the ship. The size of the compass needle is supposed so small that
P1856 we may regard the magnetic force at any point of the needle as
P1857 The iron of the ship is supposed to be of two kinds only. (1) Hard iron, magnetized in a constant manner. (2) Soft iron, the magnetization of which is induced by the earth or other magnets. In strictness we must admit that the hardest iron is not only SHIP S MAGNETISM. 71
P1858 capable of induction but that it may lose part of its so-called
P1859 permanent magnetization in various ways. The softest iron is capable of retaining what is called residual
P1860 magnetization. The actual properties of iron cannot be accurately represented by supposing it compounded of the hard iron and the soft iron above defined. But it has been found that when a ship is acted on only by the earth s magnetic force, and not subjected to any extraordinary stress of weather, the supposition that the magnetism of the ship is due partly to permanent magnetization and partly to induction leads to sufficiently accurate results when
P1861 applied to the correction of the compass.
P1862 The equations on which the theory of the variation of the compass
P1863 is founded were given by Poisson in the fifth volume of the Memoires de I Institut, p. 533 (1824).
P1864 The only assumption relative to induced magnetism which is
P1865 involved in these equations is, that if a magnetic force X due to
P1866 external magnetism produces in the iron of the ship an induced magnetization, and if this induced magnetization exerts on the
P1867 compass needle a disturbing force whose components are JT , Y 9 Z ,
P1868 then, if the external magnetic force is altered in a given ratio, the components of the disturbing force will be altered in the same ratio.
P1869 It is true that when the magnetic force acting on iron is very great the induced magnetization is no longer proportional to the external magnetic force, but this want of proportionality is quite insensible for magnetic forces of the magnitude of those due to the
P1870 Hence, in practice we may assume that if a magnetic force
P1871 whose value is unity produces through the intervention of the iron of the ship a disturbing force at the compass needle whose com
P1872 ponents are a in the direction of #, d in that of y, and g in that of z,
P1873 the components of the disturbing force due to a force X in the
P1874 direction of x will be aX, dX, and gX.
P1875 If therefore we assume axes fixed in the ship, so that x is towards
P1876 the ship s head, y to the starboard side, and z towards the keel, of the earth s magnetic and if X, Y, Z represent the components the components of the
P1877 force in these directions, and X , Y , Z
P1878 combined magnetic force of the earth and ship on the compass
P1879 needle, X = X+aX+bY+c Z+P, )
P1880 Y = Y+dX+eY+fZ+Q, (1) 72 MAGNETIC PROBLEMS. [44 1
P1881 In these equations #, #, c, d, e,f, g, h, Jc are nine constant co efficients depending on the amount, the arrangement, and the capacity for induction of the soft iron of the ship.
P1882 P, Q, and E are constant quantities depending on the permanent
P1883 magnetization of the ship. It is evident that these equations are sufficiently general if
P1884 magnetic induction is a linear function of magnetic force, for they are neither more nor less than the most general expression of a vector as a linear function of another vector.
P1885 It may also be shewn that they are not too general, for, by a proper arrangement of iron, any one of the coefficients may be made to vary independently of the others.
P1886 Thus, a long thin rod of iron under the action of a longitudinal magnetic force acquires poles, the strength of each of which is numerically equal to the cross section of the rod multiplied by the magnetizing force and by the coefficient of induced magnet
P1887 ization. A magnetic force transverse to the rod produces a much
P1888 feebler magnetization, the effect of which is almost insensible at a distance of a few diameters.
P1889 If a long iron rod be placed fore and aft with one end at a distance x from the compass needle, measured towards the ship s head, then, if the section of the rod is A, and its coefficient of
P1890 magnetization K, the strength of the pole will be A K X, and, if
P1891 A= , the force exerted by this pole on the compass needle
P1892 will be aX. The rod may be supposed so long that the effect of
P1893 the other pole on the compass may be neglected.
P1894 We have thus obtained the means of giving any required value
P1895 to the coefficient a.
P1896 If we place another rod of section B with one extremity at the
P1897 same point, distant x from the compass toward the head of the
P1898 vessel, and extending to starboard to such a distance that the
P1899 distant pole produces no sensible effect on the compass, the dis
P1900 turbing force due to this rod will be in the direction of x, and
P1901 B K.Y if B = bx* equal to x or , the force will be b Y. 2 , X K
P1902 This rod therefore introduces the coefficient b. same point will
P1903 A third rod extending downwards from the
P1904 introduce the coefficient &amp;lt;?.
P1905 The coefficients d, e,f may be produced by three rods extending to head, to starboard, and downward from a point to starboard of 44i.] SHIP S MAGNETISM. 73
P1906 the compass, and g, h, k by three rods in parallel directions from a point below the compass.
P1907 Hence each of the nine coefficients can be separately varied by means of iron rods properly placed.
P1908 The quantities P, Q, R are simply the components of the force
P1909 on the compass arising from the permanent magnetization of the ship together with that part of the induced magnetization which is due to the action of this permanent magnetization.
P1910 A complete discussion of the equations (1), and of the relation
P1911 between the true magnetic course of the ship and the course as indicated by the compass, is given by Mr. Archibald Smith in the Admiralty Manual of the Deviation of the Compass.
P1912 A valuable graphic method of investigating the problem is there
P1913 given. Taking a fixed point as origin, a line is drawn from this point representing in direction and magnitude the horizontal part of the actual magnetic force on the compass-needle. As the ship is swung round so as to bring her head into different azimuths
P1914 in succession, the extremity of this line describes a curve, each point of which corresponds to a particular azimuth.
P1915 Such a curve, by means of which the direction and magnitude of the force on the compass is given in terms of the magnetic course
P1916 of the ship, is called a Dygogram. There are two varieties of the Dygogram. In the first, the curve
P1917 is traced on a plane fixed in space as the ship turns round. In
P1918 the second kind, the curve is traced on a plane fixed with respect
P1919 The dygogram of the first kind is the Lima9on of Pascal, that of the second kind is an ellipse. For the construction and use of these curves, and for many theorems as interesting to the mathe
P1920 matician as they are important to the navigator, the reader is
P1921 referred to the Admiralty Manual of the Deviation of the Compass. CHAPTER VI.
P1922 WEBER S THEORY OF INDUCED MAGNETISM.
P1923 442.] WE have seen that Poisson supposes the magnetization of
P1924 iron to consist in a separation of the magnetic fluids within each
P1925 magnetic molecule. If we wish to avoid the assumption of the existence of magnetic fluids, we may state the same theory in another form, hy saying that each molecule of the iron, when the magnetizing force acts on it, becomes a magnet.
P1926 Weber s theory differs from this in assuming that the molecules
P1927 of the iron are always magnets, even before the application of the magnetizing force, but that in ordinary iron the magnetic axes of the molecules are turned indifferently in every direction, so that the iron as a whole exhibits no magnetic properties.
P1928 When a magnetic force acts on the iron it tends to turn the
P1929 axes of the molecules all in one direction, and so to cause the iron, as a whole, to become a magnet.
P1930 If the axes of all the molecules were set parallel to each other, the iron would exhibit the greatest intensity of magnetization of
P1931 which it is capable. Hence Weber s theory implies the existence of a limiting intensity of magnetization, and the experimental
P1932 evidence that such a limit exists is therefore necessary to the theory. Experiments shewing an approach to a limiting value of
P1933 magnetization have been made by Joule * and by J. Miiller f. The experiments of Beetz J on electrotype iron deposited under
P1934 the action of magnetic force furnish the most complete evidence of this limit,
P1935 A silver wire was varnished, and a very narrow line on the
P1936 * Annals of Electricity, iv. p. 131, 1839 ; Phil Mag. [4] ii. p. 316. t Pogg., Ann. Ixxix. p. 337, 1850. + Pogg. cxi. 1860. 443-] THE MOLECULES OF IRON ARE MAGNETS. 75
P1937 metal was laid bare by making1 a fine longitudinal scratch on the varnish. The wire was then immersed in a solution of a salt of
P1938 iron, and placed in a magnetic field with the scratch in the direction
P1939 of a line of magnetic force. By making the wire the cathode of
P1940 an electric current through the solution, iron was deposited on the narrow exposed surface of the wire, molecule by molecule. The filament of iron thus formed was then examined magnetically. P2223 Effect of Magnetization on the Dimensions of the Magnet.
P2224 448.] Joule *, in 1842, found that an iron bar becomes length
P2225 ened when it is rendered magnetic by an electric current in a
P2226 coil which surrounds it. He afterwards f shewed, by placing the
P2227 bar in water within a glass tube, that the volume of the iron is not augmented by this magnetization, and concluded that its transverse dimensions were contracted.
P2228 Finally, he passed an electric current through the axis of an iron
P2229 tube, and back outside the tube, so as to make the tube into a
P2230 closed magnetic solenoid, the magnetization being at right angles to the axis of the tube. The length of the axis of the tube was found in this case to be shortened.
P2231 He found that an iron rod under longitudinal pressure is also elongated when it is magnetized. When, however, the rod is
P2232 under considerable longitudinal tension, the effect of magnetization is to shorten it.
P2233 * Sturgeon s Annals of Electricity, vol. viii. p. 219. t Phil. Mag., 1847. 448-] CHANGE OF FORM. 87
P2234 This was the case with a wire of a quarter of an inch diameter when the tension exceeded 600 pounds weight.
P2235 In the case of a hard steel wire the effect of the magnetizing force was in every case to shorten the wire, whether the wire was under tension or pressure. The change of length lasted only as long as the magnetizing force was in action, no alteration of length was observed due to the permanent magnetization of the steel.
P2236 Joule found the elongation of iron wires to be nearly proportional to the square of the actual magnetization, so that the first effect of a demagnetizing current was to shorten the wire.
P2237 On the other hand, he found that the shortening effect on wires
P2238 under tension, and on steel, varied as the product of the magnet ization and the magnetizing current.

P2248 Since these measurements are made near the surface of the earth, the magnets are always acted on by gravity as well as by terrestrial
P2249 magnetism, and since the magnets are made of steel their mag
P2250 netism is partly permanent and partly induced. The permanent magnetism is altered by changes of temperature, by strong in duction, and by violent blows ; the induced magnetism varies with every variation of the external magnetic force.
P2251 The most convenient way of observing the force acting on a magnet is by making the magnet free to turn about a vertical axis. In ordinary compasses this is done by balancing the magnet on a vertical pivot. The finer the point of the pivot the smaller is the moment of the friction which interferes with the action of the magnetic force. For more refined observations the magnet is suspended by a thread composed of a silk fibre without twist, either single, or doubled on itself a sufficient number of times, and
P2252 so formed into a thread of parallel fibres, each of which supports as nearly as possible an equal part of the weight. The force of
P2253 torsion of such a thread is much less than that of a metal wire of equal strength, and it may be calculated in terms of the ob
P2254 served azimuth of the magnet, which is not the case with the force arising from the friction of a pivot.
P2255 The suspension fibre can be raised or lowered by turning a horizontal screw which works in a fixed nut. The fibre is wound round the thread of the screw, so that when the screw is turned the suspension fibre always hangs in the same vertical line. 450.] SUSPENSION&quot;. 89
P2256 The suspension fibre carries a small horizontal divided circle called the Torsion-circle, and a stirrup with an index, which can be placed so that the index coincides with any given division of the torsion circle. P2325 It is also advisable, especially when large angular motions have to be observed, to make the scale in the form of a concave cylindric surface, whose axis is the line of suspension. The angles are then
P2326 observed at once in circular measure without reference to a table
P2327 of tangents. The scale should be carefully adjusted, so that the axis of the cylinder coincides with the suspension fibre. The numbers on the scale should always run from the one end to the other in the same direction so as to avoid negative readings. Fig. 1 5
P2328 represents the middle portion of a scale to be used with a mirror and an inverting telescope.
P2329 This method of observation is the best when the motions are
P2330 slow. The observer sits at the telescope and sees the image of the scale moving to right or to left past the vertical wire of the telescope. With a clock beside him he can note the instant at which a given division of the scale passes the wire, or the division of the scale which is passing at a given tick of the clock, and he can also record the extreme limits of each oscillation.
P2331 When the motion is more rapid it becomes impossible to read
P2332 the divisions of the scale except at the instants of rest at the
P2333 extremities of an oscillation. A conspicuous mark may be placed
P2334 at a known division of the scale, and the instant of transit of this mark may be noted.
P2335 When the apparatus is very light, and the forces variable, the
P2336 motion is so prompt and swift that observation through a telescope METHODS OF OBSERVATION. 93
P2337 would be useless. In this case the observer looks at the scale
P2338 directly, and observes the motions of the image of the vertical wire thrown on the scale by a lamp.
P2339 It is manifest that since the image of the scale reflected by the mirror and refracted by the object glass coincides with the vertical wire, the image of the vertical wire, if sufficiently illuminated, will coincide with the scale. To observe this the room is darkened, and the concentrated rays of a lamp are thrown on the vertical wire
P2340 towards the object glass. A bright patch of light crossed by the
P2341 shadow of the wire is seen on the scale. Its motions can be
P2342 followed by the eye, and the division of the scale at which it comes to rest can be fixed on by the eye and read off at leisure. If it be desired to note the instant of the passage of the bright spot past a
P2343 given point on the scale, a pin or a bright metal wire may be
P2344 placed there so as to flash out at the time of passage.
P2345 By substituting a small hole in a diaphragm for the cross wire
P2346 the image becomes a small illuminated dot moving to right or left on the scale, and by substituting for the scale a cylinder revolving by clock work about a horizontal axis and covered with photo graphic paper, the spot of light traces out a curve which can be afterwards rendered visible. Each abscissa of this curve corresponds to a particular time, and the ordinate indicates the angular position of the mirror at that time. In this way an automatic system of continuous registration of all the elements of terrestrial
P2347 magnetism has been established at Kew and other observatories.
P2348 In some cases the telescope is dispensed with, a vertical wire is illuminated by a lamp placed behind it, and the mirror is a concave one, which forms the image of the wire on the scale as a dark line across a patch of light.

P2351 Since the scale is at the principal focus of the lens, rays from any division of the scale emerge from the lens parallel, and if the telescope is adjusted for celestial objects, it will shew the scale in optical coincidence with the cross wires of the telescope. P2531 suspended magnet hangs from a part of the apparatus which

P2534 The apparatus is first adjusted so that the axis of the telescope coincides with the mean position of the line of collimation of the magnet in its undisturbed state. If the magnet is vibrating, the true azimuth of magnetic north is found by observing the ex tremities of the oscillation of the transparent scale and making the proper correction of the reading of the azimuth circle.
P2535 The deflecting magnet is then placed upon a straight rod which passes through the axis of the revolving apparatus at right angles to the axis of the telescope, and is adjusted so that the axis of the deflecting magnet is in a line passing through the centre of the
P2536 suspended magnet. The whole of the revolving apparatus is then moved till the line
P2537 * See Airy s Magnetism. 102 MAGNETIC MEASUREMENTS. [45$.

P2539 The difference of the corrected azimuths gives the deflexion, after which we proceed as in the method of tangents, except that in the
P2540 expression for D we put sin & instead of tan 6.
P2541 In this method there is no correction for the torsion of the sus
P2542 pending fibre, since the relative position of the fibre, telescope, and magnet is the same at every observation.
P2543 The axes of the two magnets remain always at right angles in this method, so that the correction for length can be more ac curately made.
P2544 456.] Having thus measured the ratio of the moment of the deflecting magnet to the horizontal component of terrestrial mag netism, we have next to find the product of these quantities, by determining the moment of the couple with which terrestrial mag netism tends to turn the same magnet when its axis is deflected
P2545 from the magnetic meridian. There are two methods of making this measurement, the dy
P2546 namical, in which the time of vibration of the magnet under the action of terrestrial magnetism is observed, and the statical, in which the magnet is kept in equilibrium between a measurable statical couple and the magnetic force.
P2547 The dynamical method requires simpler apparatus and is more accurate for absolute measurements, but takes up a considerable time, the statical method admits of almost instantaneous measure
P2548 ment, and is therefore useful in tracing the changes of the intensity of the magnetic force, but it requires more delicate apparatus, and is not so accurate for absolute measurement.
P2549 Method of Vibrations.
P2550 The magnet is suspended with its magnetic axis horizontal, and is set in vibration in small arcs. The vibrations are observed by means of any of the methods already described.
P2551 A point on the scale is chosen corresponding to the middle of
P2552 the arc of vibration. The instant of passage through this point
P2553 of the scale in the positive direction is observed. If there is suffi
P2554 cient time before the return of the magnet to the same point, the instant of passage through the point in the negative direction is
P2555 also observed, and the process is continued till n+I positive and 456.] TIME OF VIBKATION. 103
P2556 n negative passages have been observed. If the vibrations are
P2557 too rapid to allow of every consecutive passage being observed,
P2558 every third or every fifth passage is observed, care being taken that
P2559 the observed passages are alternately positive and negative.
P2560 Let the observed times of passage be T1} T2 , T2n+1 , then if
P2561 we put I 4 + y + y 4 &c.
P2562 then Tn+1 is the mean time of the positive passages, and ought to agree with T n+v the mean time of the negative passages, if the
P2563 point has been properly chosen. The mean of these results is to be taken as the mean time of the middle passage.
P2564 After a large number of vibrations have taken place, but before the vibrations have ceased to be distinct and regular, the observer makes another series of observations, from which he deduces the mean time of the middle passage of the second series.
P2565 By calculating the period of vibration either from the first
P2566 series of observations or from the second, he ought to be able to be certain of the number of whole vibrations which have taken
P2567 place in the interval between the time of middle passage in the two series. Dividing the interval between the mean times of middle passage in the two series by this number of vibrations, the mean time of vibration is obtained.
P2568 The observed time of vibration is then to be reduced to the
P2569 time of vibration in infinitely small arcs by a formula of the same kind as that used in pendulum observations, and if the vibrations are found to diminish rapidly in amplitude, there is another cor rection for resistance, see Art. 740. These corrections, however, are very small when the magnet hangs by a fibre, and when the arc of vibration is only a few degrees.

P2586 457.] We have supposed that //and M continue constant during
P2587 the two series of experiments. The fluctuations of // may be ascertained by simultaneous observations of the bifilar magnet ometer to be presently described, and if the magnet has been in

P2592 Now the magnet when employed in the deflexion experiments
P2593 is placed with its axis east and west, so that the action of ter restrial magnetism is transverse to the magnet, and does not tend
P2594 to increase or diminish M. When the magnet is made to vibrate, its axis is north and south, so that the action of terrestrial mag
P2595 netism tends to magnetize it in the direction of the axis, and
P2596 therefore to increase its magnetic moment by a quantity Jc //, where k is a coefficient to be found by experiments on the magnet.
P2597 There are two ways in which this source of error may be avoided
P2598 without calculating Jc, the experiments being arranged so that the
P2599 magnet shall be in the same condition when employed in deflecting another magnet and when itself swinging. 457-] ELIMINATION OF INDUCTION. 105
P2600 We may place the deflecting magnet with its axis pointing
P2601 north, at a distance r from the centre of the suspended magnet, the line r making an angle whose cosine is \/J with the magnetic meridian. The action of the deflecting magnet on the suspended
P2602 one is then at right angles to its own direction, and is equal to
P2603 Here M is the magnetic moment when the axis points north,
P2604 as in the experiment of vibration, so that no correction has to be made for induction.
P2605 This method, however, is extremely difficult, owing to the large errors which would be introduced by a slight displacement of the deflecting magnet, and as the correction by reversing the deflecting magnet is not applicable here, this method is not to be followed except when the object is to determine the coefficient of induction.
P2606 The following method, in which the magnet while vibrating is freed from the inductive action of terrestrial magnetism, is due to
P2607 Two magnets are prepared whose magnetic moments are as nearly equal as possible. In the deflexion experiments these mag nets are used separately, or they may be placed simultaneously
P2608 on opposite sides of the suspended magnet to produce a greater deflexion. In these experiments the inductive force of terrestrial magnetism is transverse to the axis.
P2609 Let one of these magnets be suspended, and let the other be placed parallel to it with its centre exactly below that of the sus pended magnet, and with its axis in the same direction. The force which the fixed magnet exerts on the suspended one is in the opposite direction from that of terrestrial magnetism. If the fixed magnet be gradually brought nearer to the suspended one the time
P2610 of vibration will increase, till at a certain point the equilibrium will
P2611 cease to be stable, and beyond this point the suspended magnet will make oscillations in the reverse position. By experimenting in this way a position of the fixed magnet is found at which it exactly neutralizes the effect of terrestrial magnetism on the sus pended one. The two magnets are fastened together so as to be parallel, with their axes turned the same way, and at the distance just found by experiment. They are then suspended in the usual way and made to vibrate together through small arcs.

P2615 moment, the upper one neutralizes the inductive action of the earth
P2616 M on the lower one. is therefore the same in the experiment of

P2735 460.] In an observatory, where a continuous system of regis
P2736 tration of declination and intensity is kept up either by eye ob
P2737 servation or by the automatic photographic method, the absolute
P2738 values of the declination and of the intensity, as well as the position
P2739 and moment of the magnetic axis of a magnet, may be determined
P2740 to a greater degree of accuracy.
P2741 For the declinometer gives the declination at every instant affected
P2742 by a constant error, and the bifilar magnetometer gives the intensity
P2743 at every instant multiplied by a constant coefficient. In the ex
P2744 periments we substitute for b, 8 + 8 where 8 is the reading of
P2745 the declinometer at the given instant, and 8 is the unknown but
P2746 constant error, so that 8 + 8 is the true declination at that instant.
P2747 In like manner for H, we substitute CH where IF is the readi&quot; ngO*
P2748 of the magnetometer on its arbitrary scale, and C is an unknown
P2749 but constant multiplier which converts these readings into absolute
P2750 measure, so that CH is the horizontal force at a given instant.
P2751 The experiments to determine the absolute values of the quan
P2752 tities must be conducted at a sufficient distance from the declino
P2753 meter and magnetometer, so that the different magnets may not
P2754 H sensibly disturb each other. The time of every observation must be noted and the corresponding values of 8 and inserted. The
P2755 equations are then to be treated so as to find 8 , the constant error
P2756 of the declinometer, and C the coefficient to be applied to the readings of the magnetometer. When these are found the readings
P2757 of both instruments may be expressed in absolute measure. The
P2758 absolute measurements, however, must be frequently repeated in
P2759 order to take account of changes which may occur in the magnetic axis and magnetic moment of the magnets.
P2760 461.] The methods of determining the vertical component of the
P2761 terrestrial magnetic force have not been brought to the same degree
P2762 of precision. The vertical force must act on a magnet which turns
P2763 about a horizontal axis. Now a body which turns about a hori
P2764 zontal axis cannot be made so sensitive to the action of small forces
P2765 as a body which is suspended by a fibre and turns about a vertical axis. Besides this, the weight of a magnet is so large compared 112 MAGNETIC MEASUREMENTS. [461.
P2766 with the magnetic force exerted upon it that a small displace ment of the centre of inertia by unequal dilatation, &c. produces a greater effect on the position of the magnet than a considerable change of the magnetic force.
P2767 Hence the measurement of the vertical force, or the comparison of the vertical and the horizontal forces, is the least perfect part of the system of magnetic measurements.
P2768 The vertical part of the magnetic force is generally deduced from the horizontal force by determining the direction of the total force.
P2769 H If i be the angle which the total force makes with its horizontal
P2770 component, i is called the magnetic Dip or Inclination, and if
P2771 H is the horizontal force already found, then the vertical force is
P2772 //tan i, and the total force is sec i. The magnetic dip is found by means of the Dip Needle. The theoretical dip-needle is a magnet with an axis which passes
P2773 through its centre of inertia perpendicular to the magnetic axis of the needle. The ends of this axis are made in the form of
P2774 cylinders of small radius, the axes of which are coincident with the line passing through the centre of inertia. These cylindrical ends rest on two horizontal planes and are free to roll on them.
P2775 When the axis is placed magnetic east and west, the needle
P2776 is free to rotate in the plane of the magnetic meridian, and if the instrument is in perfect adjustment, the magnetic axis will set itself in the direction of the total magnetic force.
P2777 It is, however, practically impossible to adjust a dip-needle so that its weight does not influence its position of equilibrium, because its centre of inertia, even if originally in the line joining the centres of the rolling sections of the cylindrical ends, will cease
P2778 to be in this line when the needle is imperceptibly bent or un
P2779 equally expanded. Besides, the determination of the true centre
P2780 of inertia of a magnet is a very difficult operation, owing to the interference of the magnetic force with that of gravity.

P2924 At several observatories photographic traces are now produced on prepared paper moved by clock work, so that a continuous record
P2925 of the indications of the three instruments at every instant is formed.

The declinometer gives the force towards mean magnetic west, the bifilar magnet ometer gives the variation of the force towards magnetic north, and the balance magnetometer gives the variation of the vertical force. P2927 instruments indicate their several zeros, are deduced by frequent observations of the absolute declination, horizontal force, and dip. CHAPTER VIII.
P2928 ON TERRESTRIAL MAGNETISM.
P2929 465.] OUR knowledge of Terrestrial Magnetism is derived from
P2930 the study of the distribution of magnetic force on the earth s sur face at any one time, and of the changes in that distribution at
P2931 The magnetic force at any one place and time is known when its three coordinates are known. These coordinates may be given
P2932 in the form of the declination or azimuth of the force, the dip
P2933 or inclination to the horizon, and the total intensity. The most convenient method, however, for investigating the
P2934 general distribution of magnetic force on the earth s surface is to
P2935 consider the magnitudes of the three components of the force,

P2940 where I is the latitude, and A. the longitude, and r the distance from the centre of the earth.

P2942 from the observations of horizontal force alone as follows.
P2943 Let FQ be the value of V at the true north pole, then, taking
P2944 the line-integral along any meridian, we find,
P2945 for the value of the potential on that meridian at latitude I. 466.] MAGNETIC SURVEY. 121

P2947 at every point, and F , the value of Fat the pole. Since the forces depend not on the absolute value of V but
P2948 on its derivatives, it is not necessary to fix any particular value

P3024 If we call the points where the potential is a minimum true north poles, then these other points may be called false north poles, because the compass-needle is not true to them. If there are p true north poles, there must be p I false north poles, and in like
P3025 manner, if there are q true south poles, there must be y 1 false south poles. The number of poles of the same name must be odd, so that the opinion at one time prevalent, that there are two north poles and two south poles, is erroneous. According to Gauss there is in fact only one true north pole and one true south pole on the earth s surface, and therefore there are no false poles. The line joining these poles is not a diameter of the earth, and it is not parallel to the earth s magnetic axis.
P3026 469.] Most of the early investigators into the nature of the earth s magnetism endeavoured to express it as the result of the action of one or more bar magnets, the position of the poles of which were to be determined. Gauss was the first to express the distribution of the earth s magnetism in a perfectly general way by expanding its potential in a series of solid harmonics, the coefficients of which he determined for the first four degrees. These coeffi cients are 24 in number, 3 for the first degree, 5 for the second, 7 for the third, and 9 for the fourth. All these terms are found
P3027 necessary in order to give a tolerably accurate representation of the actual state of the earth s magnetism.

P3049 attention has been directed are as follows.
P3050 * Professor Hornstein of Prague has discovered a periodic change in the magnetic elements, the period of which is 26.33 days, almost exactly equal to that of the synodic revolution of the sun, as deduced from the observation of sun-spots near his equator. This method of discovering the time of rotation of the unseen solid body of the sun by its effects on the magnetic needle is the first instalment of the repayment by Magnetism of its debt to Astronomy. Akad., Wien, June 1,5, 1871. See Proc. R.8., Nov. 16,1871. 126 TERRESTRIAL MAGNETISM. [473
P3051 I. The more Regular Variations.
P3052 (1) The Solar variations, depending on the hour of the day and the time of the year.
P3053 (2) The Lunar variations, depending on the moon s hour angle
P3054 and on her other elements of position. (3) These variations do not repeat themselves in different years,,
P3055 but seem to be subject to a variation of longer period of about eleven years.
P3056 (4) Besides this, there is a secular alteration in the state of the earth s magnetism, which has been going on ever since magnetic observations have been made, and is producing changes of the magnetic elements of far greater magnitude than any of the varia tions of small period.
P3057 II. The Disturbances.
P3058 473.] Besides the more regular changes, the magnetic elements are subject to sudden disturbances of greater or less amount. It is found that these disturbances are more powerful and frequent at one time than at another, and that at times of great disturbance the laws of the regular variations are masked, though they are very distinct at times of small disturbance. Hence great attention has been paid to these disturbances, and it has been found that dis turbances of a particular kind are more likely to occur at certain times of the day, and at certain seasons and intervals of time, though each individual disturbance appears quite irregular. Besides these more ordinary disturbances, there are occasionally times of excessive disturbance, in which the magnetism is strongly disturbed for a day or two. These are called Magnetic Storms. Individual disturbances have been sometimes observed at the same instant
P3059 in stations widely distant. Mr. Airy has found that a large proportion of the disturbances
P3060 at Greenwich correspond with the electric currents collected by electrodes placed in the earth in the neighbourhood, and are such as would be directly produced in the magnet if the earth-current, retaining its actual direction, were conducted through a wire placed underneath the magnet.
P3061 It has been found that there is an epoch of maximum disturbance
P3062 every eleven years, and that this appears to coincide with the epoch
P3063 of maximum number of spots in the sun. 474.] The field of investigation into which we are introduced 474-] VARIATIONS AND DISTURBANCES. 127
P3064 by the study of terrestrial magnetism is as profound as it is ex
P3065 We know that the sun and moon act on the earth s magnetism.
P3066 It has been proved that this action cannot be explained by sup posing these bodies magnets. The action is therefore indirect. In
P3067 the case of the sun part of it may be thermal action, but in the case of the moon we cannot attribute it to this cause. Is it pos
P3068 sible that the attraction of these bodies, by causing strains in the interior of the earth, produces (Art. 447) changes in the magnetism already existing in the earth, and so by a kind of tidal action causes
P3069 the semidiurnal variations ?
P3070 But the amount of all these changes is very small compared with the great secular changes of the earth s magnetism.
P3071 What cause, whether exterior to the earth or in its inner depth s,
P3072 produces such enormous changes in the earth s magnetism, that its magnetic poles move slowly from one part of the globe to another ?
P3073 When we consider that the intensity of the magnetization of the
P3074 great globe of the earth is quite comparable with that which we produce with much difficulty in our steel magnets, these immense changes in so large a body force us to conclude that we are not yet acquainted with one of the most powerful agents in nature,, the scene of whose activity lies in those inner depths of the earth, to the knowledge of which we have so few means of access. PART IV.
P3075 ELECTROMAGNETIC FORCE.
P3076 475.] IT had been noticed by many different observers that in
P3077 certain cases magnetism is produced or destroyed in needles by electric discharges through them or near them, and conjectures
P3078 of various kinds had been made as to the relation between mag
P3079 netism and electricity, but the laws of these phenomena, and the
P3080 form of these relations, remained entirely unknown till Hans
P3081 Christian Orsted *, at a private lecture to a few advanced students at Copenhagen, observed that a wire connecting the ends of a voltaic battery affected a magnet in its vicinity. This discovery he published in a tract entitled Experiments circa effectum Conflictus
P3082 Electrici in Acum Magneticam, dated July 21, 1820. Experiments on the relation of the magnet to bodies charged
P3083 with electricity had been tried without any result till Orsted endeavoured to ascertain the effect of a wire heated by an electric
P3084 current. He discovered, however, that the current itself, and not
P3085 the heat of the wire, was the cause of the action, and that the e electric conflict acts in a revolving manner, that is, that a magnet placed near a wire transmitting an electric current tends to set itself perpendicular to the wire, and with the same end always pointing forwards as the magnet is moved round the wire.
P3086 476.] It appears therefore that in the space surrounding a wire

P3145 482.] In all actual experiments the current forms a closed circuit

P3150 and whose magnetic moment is equal to the area of the circuit

P3152 by the circuit and thus forming a diaphragm, and if a magnetic shell of strength i coinciding with this surface be substituted for the electric current, then the magnetic action of the shell on all
P3153 distant points will be identical with that of the current.

P3197 486.] If a long thin flexible solenoidal magnet were placed in the neighbourhood of an electric circuit, the north and south ends of the solenoid would tend to move in opposite directions round the wire, and if they were free to obey the magnetic force the magnet would finally become wound round the wire in a close coil. If it were possible to obtain a magnet having only one pole, or poles of unequal strength, such a magnet would be moved round and round the wire continually in one direction, but since the poles of every magnet are equal and opposite, this result can never occur. Faraday, however, has shewn how to produce the continuous rotation of one pole of a magnet round an electric current by making it possible for one pole to go round and round the current while the other pole does not. P3377 If on one side of the wire which carries the current the magnetic action due to the current is in the same or nearly the same direction as that due to other currents, then, on the other side of the wire, these forces will be in opposite or nearly opposite directions, and the force acting on the wire will be from the side on which the forces strengthen each other to the side on which they oppose each
P3378 Thus, if a descending current is placed in a field of magnetic force directed towards the north, its magnetic action will be to the north on the west side, and to the south on the east side. P3379 other on the east side, and the current will therefore be acted

P3387 netic force due to any short portion of a second current in the same plane. If the second current is on the right side of the first, the magnetic forces will strengthen each other on its right side and oppose each other on its left side. Hence the second current will be acted on by a force urging it from its right side to its left side. The magnitude of this force depends only on the position of the second current and not on its direction. If the second current is
P3388 on the left side of the first it will be urged from left to right. Hence, if the second current is in the same direction as the first
P3389 it is attracted, if in the opposite direction it is repelled, if it flows
