P7850 ELECTROMAGNETIC UNIT OF RESISTANCE.
P7851 On the Determination of the Resistance of a Coil in Electro
P7852 758.] THE resistance of a conductor is defined as the ratio of the
P7853 numerical value of the electromotive force to that of the current
P7854 which it produces in the conductor. The determination of the value of the current in electromagnetic measure can be made by means of a standard galvanometer, when we know the value of the earth s magnetic force. The determination of the value of the electromotive force is more difficult, as the only case in which we can directly calculate its value is when it arises from the relative motion of the circuit with respect to a known magnetic system.
P7855 759.] The first determination of the resistance of a wire in
P7856 electromagnetic measure was made by Kirchhoff*. He employed two coils of known form, A1 and A^ and calculated their coefficient
P7857 of mutual induction from the geo metrical data of their form and
P7858 position. These coils were placed in circuit with a galvanometer, 6r, and a battery, B, and two points of the circuit, P, between the coils, and Q, between the battery and galvanometer, were joined by the wire whose resistance, R, was to be measured.

P7860 the galvanometer circuit, and produces a certain permanent de
P7861 flexion of the galvanometer. If the coil A1 is now removed quickly

P7916 761.] To make a series of observations Weber began with the coil parallel to the magnetic meridian. P7920 ciently large deflexions, to make the wire of copper, a metal which, though it is the best conductor, has the disadvantage of altering
P7921 considerably in resistance with alterations of temperature. It is also very difficult to ascertain the temperature of every part of the apparatus. Hence, in order to obtain a result of permanent value from such an experiment, the resistance of the experimental circuit should be compared with that of a carefully constructed resistance coil, both before and after each experiment.

P8087 Determination of the Number of Electrostatic Units of Electricity
P8088 in one Electromagnetic Unit.
P8089 768.] THE absolute magnitudes of the electrical units in both systems depend on the units of length, time, and mass which we adopt, and the mode in which they depend on these units is different in the two systems, so that the ratio of the electrical units will be expressed by a different number, according to the different
P8090 units of length and time. It appears from the table of dimensions, Art. 628, that the
P8091 number of electrostatic units of electricity in one electromagnetic unit varies inversely as the magnitude of the unit of length, and directly as the magnitude of the unit of time which we adopt.
P8092 If, therefore, we determine a velocity which is represented nu merically by this number, then, even if we adopt new units of length and of time, the number representing this velocity will still be the number of electrostatic units of electricity in one electro magnetic unit, according to the new system of measurement.
P8093 This velocity, therefore, which indicates the relation between electrostatic and electromagnetic phenomena, is a natural quantity of definite magnitude, and the measurement of this quantity is one of the most important researches in electricity.
P8094 To shew that the quantity we are in search of is really a velocity, we may observe that in the case of two parallel currents the attrac tion experienced by a length a of one of them is, by Art. 686,

P8138 I. Comparison of Units of Electricity.
P8139 771.] Since the ratio of the electromagnetic to the electrostatic
P8140 unit of electricity is represented by a velocity, we shall in future denote it by the symbol v. The first numerical determination of this velocity was made by Weber and Kohlrausch *.
P8141 Their method was founded on the measurement of the same
P8142 quantity of electricity, first in electrostatic and then in electro magnetic measure.
P8143 The quantity of electricity measured was the charge of a Leyden jar. It was measured in electrostatic measure as the product of the
P8144 * Elektrodynamische Maasbestimmungen ; and Pogg. Ann. xcix, (Aug. 10, 1856.) 77I-] METHOD OF WEBER AND KOHLRAUSCH. 371
P8145 capacity of the jar into the difference of potential of its coatings.
P8146 The capacity of the jar was determined by comparison with that of a sphere suspended in an open space at a distance from other bodies. The capacity of such a sphere is expressed in electrostatic
P8147 measure by its radius. Thus the capacity of the jar may be found
P8148 and expressed as a certain length. See Art. 227.
P8149 The difference of the potentials of the coatings of the jar was mea
P8150 sured by connecting the coatings with the electrodes of an electro meter, the constants of which were carefully determined, so that the difference of the potentials, U, became known in electrostatic measure.
P8151 By multiplying this by c, the capacity of the jar, the charge of
P8152 the jar was expressed in electrostatic measure. To determine the value of the charge in electromagnetic measure,
P8153 the jar was discharged through the coil of a galvanometer. The
P8154 effect of the transient current on the magnet of the galvanometer communicated to the magnet a certain angular velocity. The magnet then swung round to a certain deviation, at which its velocity was entirely destroyed by the opposing action of the
P8155 By observing the extreme deviation of the magnet the quantity of electricity in the current may be determined in electromagnetic
P8156 measure, as in Art. 748, by the formula
P8157 T // 2 sin i&amp;lt;9,
P8158 where Q is the quantity of electricity in electromagnetic measure.
P8159 We have therefore to determine the following quantities :
P8160 U, the intensity of the horizontal component of terrestrial mag
P8161 netism ; see Art. 456.
P8162 G, the principal constant of the galvanometer; see Art. 700. T, the time of a single vibration of the magnet ; and 6, the deviation due to the transient current.
P8163 The value of v obtained by MM. Weber and Kohlrausch was
P8164 v 310740000 metres per second.
P8165 The property of solid dielectrics, to which the name of Electric
P8166 Absorption has been given, renders it difficult to estimate correctly
P8167 the capacity of a Leyden jar. The apparent capacity varies ac cording to the time which elapses between the charging or dis charging of the jar and the measurement of the potential, and the
P8168 longer the time the greater is the value obtained for the capacity of the jar.

P8175 II. v expressed as a Resistance,
P8176 772. J Two other methods for the determination of v lead to an
P8177 expression of its value in terms of the resistance of a given con ductor, which, in the electromagnetic system, is also expressed as a
P8178 In Sir William Thomson s form of the experiment, a constant current is made to flow through a wire of great resistance. The electromotive force which urges the current through the wire is mea sured electrostatically by connecting the extremities of the wire with the electrodes of an absolute electrometer, Arts. 217, 218. The strength of the current in the wire is measured in electromagnetic measure by the deflexion of the suspended coil of an electrodyna mometer through which it passes, Art. 725. The resistance of the circuit is known in electromagnetic measure by comparison with a
P8179 standard coil or Ohm. By multiplying the strength of the current
P8180 by this resistance we obtain the electromotive force in electro magnetic measure, and from a comparison of this with the electro
P8181 static measure the value of v is obtained.
P8182 This method requires the simultaneous determination of two forces, by means of the electrometer and electrodynamometer re spectively, and it is only the ratio of these forces which appears in
P8183 773.] Another method, in which these forces, instead of being
P8184 separately measured, are directly opposed to each other, was em
P8185 ployed by the present writer. The ends of the great resistance coil are connected with two parallel disks, one of which is moveable. The same difference of potentials which sends the current through the great resistance, also causes an attraction between these disks. At the same time, an electric current which, in the actual experi ment, was distinct from the primary current, is sent through two coils, fastened, one to the back of the fixed disk, and the other to the back of the moveable disk. The current flows in opposite
P8186 directions through these coils, so that they repel one another. By
P8187 adjusting the distance of the two disks the attraction is exactly balanced by the repulsion, while at the same time another observer, 774-] METHODS OF THOMSON AND MAXWELL. 373
P8188 by means of a differential galvanometer with shunts, determines the ratio of the primary to the secondary current.
P8189 In this experiment the only measurement which must he referred to a material standard is that of the great resistance, which must
P8190 be determined in absolute measure by comparison with the Ohm. The other measurements are required only for the determination of ratios, and may therefore be determined in terms of any arbitrary
P8191 Thus the ratio of the two forces is a ratio of equality. The ratio of the two currents is found by a comparison of resist ances when there is no deflexion of the differential galvanometer. The attractive force depends on the square of the ratio of the diameter of the disks to their distance.
P8192 The repulsive force depends on the ratio of the diameter of the
P8193 coils to their distance.
P8194 The value of v is therefore expressed directly in terms of the resistance of the great coil, which is itself compared with the Ohm.
P8195 The value oft?, as found by Thomson s method, was 28.2 Ohms* ;
P8196 by Maxwell s, 28.8 Ohmsf.

P8416 ELECTROMAGNETIC THEORY OF LIGHT.
P8417 781.] IN several parts of this treatise an attempt has been made to explain electromagnetic phenomena by means of mechanical action transmitted from one body to another by means of a medium occupying the space between them. The undulatory theory of light
P8418 also assumes the existence of a medium. We have now to shew
P8419 that the properties of the electromagnetic medium are identical with those of the luminiferous medium.
P8420 To fill all space with a new medium whenever any new phe nomenon is to be explained is by no means philosophical, but if the study of two different branches of science has independently suggested the idea of a medium, and if the properties which must be attributed to the medium in order to account for electro magnetic phenomena are of the same kind as those which we attribute to the luminiferous medium in order to account for the
P8421 phenomena of light, the evidence for the physical existence of the medium will be considerably strengthened.
P8422 But the properties of bodies are capable of quantitative measure
P8423 ment. We therefore obtain the numerical value of some property of
P8424 the medium, such as the velocity with which a disturbance is pro pagated through it, which can be calculated from electromagnetic experiments, and also observed directly in the case of light. If it should be found that the velocity of propagation of electromagnetic disturbances is the same as the velocity of light, and this not only in air, but in other transparent media, we shall have strong reasons for believing that light is an electromagnetic phenomenon, and the combination of the optical with the electrical evidence will produce a conviction of the reality of the medium similar to that which we obtain, in the case of other kinds of matter, from the combined
P8425 evidence of the senses. 384 ELECTROMAGNETIC THEORY OF LIGHT.
P8426 782.] When light is emitted, a certain amount of energy is
P8427 expended by the luminous body, and if the light is absorbed by another body, this body becomes heated, shewing that it has re ceived energy from without. During the interval of time after the light left the first body and before it reached the second, it must have existed as energy in the intervening space.
P8428 According to the theory of emission, the transmission of energy is effected by the actual transference of light-corpuscules from the luminous to the illuminated body,, carrying with them their kinetic
P8429 energy, together with any other kind of energy of which they may
P8430 According to the theory of undulation, there is a material medium which fills the space between the two bodies, and it is by the action of contiguous parts of this medium that the energy is passed on,
P8431 from one portion to the next, till it reaches the illuminated body.
P8432 The luminiferous medium is therefore, during the passage of light
P8433 through it, a receptacle of energy. In the undulatory theory, as
P8434 developed by Huygens, Fresnel, Young, Green, &c., this energy is supposed to be partly potential and partly kinetic. The potential
P8435 energy is supposed to be due to the distortion of the elementary
P8436 portions of the medium. We must therefore regard the medium as
P8437 elastic. The kinetic energy is supposed to be due to the vibratory
P8438 motion of the medium. We must therefore regard the medium as
P8439 having a finite density. In the theory of electricity and magnetism adopted in this
P8440 treatise, two forms of energy are recognised, the electrostatic and the electrokinetic (see Arts. 630 and 636), and these are supposed
P8441 to have their seat, not merely in the electrified or magnetized bodies, but in every part of the surrounding space, where electric or magnetic force is observed to act. Hence our theory agTees
P8442 with the undulatory theory in assuming the existence of a medium which is capable of becoming a receptacle of two forms of energy *.
P8443 783.] Let us next determine the conditions of the propagation of an electromagnetic disturbance through a uniform medium, which we shall suppose to be at rest, that is, to have no motion except that
P8444 which may be involved in electromagnetic disturbances.
P8445 * For my own part, considering the relation of a vacuum to the magnetic force, and the general character of magnetic phenomena external to the magnet, I am more
P8446 inclined to the notion that in the transmission of the force there is such an action, external to the magnet, than that the effects are merely attraction and repulsion at a
P8447 distance. Such an action may be a function of the aether; for it is not at all unlikely
P8448 that, if there be an aether, it should have other uses than simply the conveyance of radiations. Faraday s Experimental Researches, 3075. 783-] PROPAGATION OF ELECTROMAGNETIC DISTURBANCES. 385

P8723 greater the velocity will appear to be, for however great the distance, and however small the time, the value of the disturbance will differ
P8724 mathematically from zero. This peculiarity of diffusion distinguishes it from wave-propaga
P8725 tion, which takes place with a definite velocity. No disturbance

P8730 When the current begins, its first effect is to produce a current
P8731 of induction in the parts of the medium close to the wire. The
P8732 direction of this current is opposite to that of the original current, and in the first instant its total quantity is equal to that of the
P8733 original current, so that the electromagnetic effect on more distant parts of the medium is initially zero, and only rises to its final value as the induction-current dies away on account of the electric resistance of the medium.
P8734 But as the induction-current close to the wire dies away, a new induction -current is generated in the medium beyond, so that the
P8735 space occupied by the induction-current is continually becoming

P8737 menon precisely analogous to the diffusion of heat from a part of 398 ELECTROMAGNETIC THEORY OF LIGHT. [805.

P8761 806.] THE most important step in establishing a relation between electric and magnetic phenomena and those of light must be the discovery of some instance in which the one set of phenomena is aifected by the other. In the search for such phenomena we must be guided by any knowledge we may have already obtained with
P8762 respect to the mathematical or geometrical form of the quantities
P8763 which we wish to compare. Thus, if we endeavour, as Mrs. Somer ville did, to magnetize a needle by means of light, we must re member that the distinction between magnetic north and south is a mere matter of direction, and would be at once reversed if we
P8764 reverse certain conventions about the use of mathematical signs. There is nothing in magnetism analogous to those phenomena of electrolysis which enable us to distinguish positive from negative electricity, by observing that oxygen appears at one pole of a cell and hydrogen at the other.
P8765 Hence we must not expect that if we make light fall on one end of a needle, that end will become a pole of a certain name, for the two poles do not differ as light does from darkness.
P8766 We might expect a better result if we caused circularly polarized
P8767 light to fall on the needle, right-handed light falling on one end and left-handed on the other, for in some respects these kinds of
P8768 light may be said to be related to each other in the same way as the poles of a magnet. The analogy, however, is faulty even here, for the two rays when combined do not neutralize each other, but
P8769 produce a plane polarized ray.
P8770 Faraday, who was acquainted with the method of studying the strains produced in transparent solids by means of polarized light, made many experiments in hopes of detecting some action on polar ized light while passing through a medium in which electrolytic conduction or dielectric induction exists *. He was not, however,

P8818 It is a well-known theorem in kinematics that two uniform cir
P8819 cular vibrations, of the same amplitude, having the same periodic time, and in the same plane, but revolving in opposite directions, are equivalent, when compounded together, to a rectilinear vibra tion. The periodic time of this vibration is equal to that of the circular vibrations, its amplitude is double, and its direction is in the line joining the points at which two particles, describing the circular vibrations in opposite directions round the same circle, would meet. Hence if one of the circular vibrations has its phase accelerated, the direction of the rectilinear vibration will be turned,
P8820 in the same direction as that of the circular vibration, through an
P8821 angle equal to half the acceleration of phase.
P8822 It can also be proved by direct optical experiment that two rays of light, circularly-polarized in opposite directions, and of the same intensity, become, when united, a plane-polarized ray, and that if by any means the phase of one of the circularly-polarized rays is
P8823 accelerated, the plane of polarization of the resultant ray is turned round half the angle of acceleration of the phase.

P8926 In the case of circularly-polarized light, the magnitude of this vector remains always the same, but its direction rotates round the direction of the ray so as to complete a revolution in the periodic time of the wave. The uncertainty which exists as to whether this vector is in the plane of polarization or perpendicular to it, does not extend to our knowledge of the direction in which it rotates in right handed and in left-handed circularly-polarized light respectively. The direction and the angular velocity of this vector are perfectly known, though the physical nature of the vector and its absolute direction at a given instant are uncertain.
P8927 When a ray of circularly-polarized light falls on a medium under
P8928 the action of magnetic force, its propagation within the medium is affected by the relation of the direction of rotation of the light to 408 MAGNETIC ACTION ON LIGHT. [822.
P8929 the direction of the magnetic force. From this we conclude, by the reasoning of Art. 821, that in the medium, when under the action of magnetic force, some rotatory motion is going on, the axis of ro
P8930 tation being in the direction of the magnetic forces ; and that the rate of propagation of circularly-polarized light, when the direction of its vibratory rotation and the direction of the magnetic rotation of the medium are the same, is different from the rate of propaga tion when these directions are opposite.
P8931 The only resemblance which we can trace between a medium through which circularly-polarized light is propagated, and a me dium through which lines of magnetic force pass, is that in both there is a motion of rotation about an axis. But here the resem
P8932 blance stops, for the rotation in the optical phenomenon is that of the vector which represents^ the disturbance. This vector is always perpendicular to the direction of the ray, and rotates about it a known number of times in a second. In the magnetic phenomenon, that which rotates has no properties by which its sides can be dis tinguished, so that we cannot determine how many times it rotates
P8933 There is nothing, therefore, in the magnetic phenomenon which corresponds to the wave-length and the wave-propagation in the op
P8934 tical phenomenon. A medium in which a constant magnetic force
P8935 is acting is not, in consequence of that force, filled with waves
P8936 travelling in one direction, as when light is propagated through it. The only resemblance between the optical and the magnetic pheno menon is, that at each point of the medium something exists of
P8937 the nature of an angular velocity about an axis in the direction of the magnetic force.

P9086 831.] NOTE. The whole of this chapter may be regarded as an
P9087 expansion of the exceedingly important remark of Sir William Thomson in the Proceedings of the Royal Society, June 1856 : The magnetic influence on light discovered by Faraday depends on the direction of motion of moving particles. For instance, in a medium possessing it, particles in a straight line parallel to the lines of magnetic force, displaced to a helix round this line as axis, and then projected tangentially with such velocities as to describe circles, will have different velocities according as their motions are round in one direction (the same as the nominal direction of the galvanic current in the magnetizing coil), or in the contrary direction. But
P9088 the elastic reaction of the medium must be the same for the same
P9089 displacements, whatever be the velocities and directions of the par ticles ; that is to say, the forces which are balanced by centrifugal force of the circular motions are equal, while the luminiferous motions are unequal. The absolute circular motions being there fore either equal or such as to transmit equal centrifugal forces to the particles initially considered, it follows that the luminiferous motions are only components of the whole motion ; and that a less
P9090 luminiferous component in one direction, compounded with a mo tion existing in the medium when transmitting no light, skives an
P9091 equal resultant to that of a greater luminiferous motion in the con trary direction compounded with the same non -luminous motion. I think it is not only impossible to conceive any other than this 410 MAGNETIC ACTION ON LIGHT.
P9092 dynamical explanation of the fact that circularly-polarized light
P9093 transmitted through magnetized glass parallel to the lines of mag
P9094 netizing force, with the same quality, right-handed always, or left
P9095 handed always, is propagated at different rates according as its
P9096 course is in the direction or is contrary to the direction in which a
P9097 north magnetic pole is drawn ; but I believe it can be demonstrated
P9098 that no other explanation of that fact is possible. Hence it appears
P9099 that Faraday s optical discovery affords a demonstration of the re
P9100 ality of Ampere s explanation of the ultimate nature of magnetism ;
P9101 and gives a definition of magnetization in the dynamical theory of
P9102 heat. The introduction of the principle of moments of momenta
P9103 the conservation of areas into the mechanical treatment of

P9105 of molecular vortices,&quot;
P9106 cate a line perpendicular to the plane of resultant rotatory mo
P9107 mentum (&quot;the invariable plane&quot;) of the thermal motions as the

P9109 moment of momenta of these motions as the definite measure of
P9110 &quot; The explanation of all phenomena of
P9111 the magnetic moment.&quot;
P9112 electromagnetic attraction or repulsion, and of electromagnetic in
P9113 duction, is to be looked for simply in the inertia and pressure of
P9114 the matter of which the motions constitute heat. Whether this
P9115 matter is or is not electricity, whether it is a continuous fluid inter
P9116 permeating the spaces between molecular nuclei, or is itself mole cularly grouped ; or whether all matter is continuous, and molecular
P9117 heterogeneousness consists in finite vortical or other relative mo
P9118 tions of contiguous parts of a body ; it is impossible to decide, and perhaps in vain to speculate, in the present state of science.
P9119 A theory of molecular vortices, which I worked out at consider
P9120 able length, was published in the Phil. Mag. for March, April, and May, 1861, Jan. and Feb. 1862.
P9121 I think we have good evidence for the opinion that some pheno menon of rotation is going on in the magnetic field, that this rota tion is performed by a great number of very small portions of matter, each rotating on its own axis, this axis being parallel to the direction of the magnetic force, and that the rotations of these dif ferent vortices are made to depend on one another by means of some kind of mechanism connecting them.
P9122 The attempt which I then made to imagine a working model of this mechanism must be taken for no more than it really is, a de monstration that mechanism may be imagined capable of producing
P9123 a connexion mechanically equivalent to the actual connexion of the 831.] THEOBY OP MOLECULAK VORTICES. 417
P9124 parts of the electromagnetic field. The problem of determining the mechanism required to establish a given species of connexion be tween the motions of the parts of a system always admits of an
P9125 infinite number of solutions. Of these, some may be more clumsy
P9126 or more complex than others, but all must satisfy the conditions of mechanism in general.

P9129 (2) Electromagnetic induction of currents is the effect of the

P9131 (4) Electric displacement arises from the elastic yielding of the connecting mechanism.

P9135 832.] WE have seen (Art. 380) that the action of magnets on
P9136 one another can be accurately represented by the attractions and
P9137 repulsions of an imaginary substance called * matter.
P9138 We have shewn the reasons why we must not suppose this magnetic
P9139 matter to move from one part of a magnet to another through a
P9140 sensible distance, as at first sight it appears to do when we
P9141 magnetize a bar, and we were led to Poisson s hypothesis that the
P9142 magnetic matter is strictly confined to single molecules oi&quot; the mag
P9143 netic substance, so that a magnetized molecule is one in which the opposite kinds of magnetic matter are more or less separated to
P9144 wards opposite poles of the molecule, but so that no part of either
P9145 can ever be actually separated from the molecule (Art. 430).
P9146 These arguments completely establish the fact, that magnetiza
P9147 tion is a phenomenon, not of large masses of iron, but of molecules, that is to say, of portions of the substance so small that we cannot by any mechanical method cut one of them in two, so as to obtain a
P9148 north pole separate from a south pole. But the nature of a mag
P9149 netic molecule is by no means determined without further investi
P9150 gation. We have seen (Art. 442) that there are strong reasons for
P9151 believing that the act of magnetizing iron or steel does not consist in imparting magnetization to the molecules of which it is com
P9152 posed, but that these molecules are already magnetic, even in un
P9153 magnetized iron, but with their axes placed indifferently in all
P9154 directions, and that the act of magnetization consists in turning
P9155 the molecules so that their axes are either rendered all parallel to
P9156 one direction, or at least. are deflected towards that direction. 834-] AMPERE S THEORY. 419
P9157 833.] Still, however, we have arrived at no explanation of the nature of a magnetic molecule, that is, we have not recognized its
P9158 likeness to any other thing of which we know more. We have
P9159 therefore to consider the hypothesis of Ampere, that the magnetism of the molecule is due to an electric current constantly circulating in some closed path within it.
P9160 It is possible to produce an exact imitation of the action of any magnet on points external to it, by means of a sheet of electric currents properly distributed on its outer surface. But the action of the magnet on points in the interior is quite different from the
P9161 action of the electric currents on corresponding points. Hence Am
P9162 pere concluded that if magnetism is to be explained by means of electric currents, these currents must circulate within the molecules
P9163 of the magnet, and must not flow from one molecule to another. As we cannot experimentally measure the magnetic action at a point in the interior of a molecule, this hypothesis cannot be dis proved in the same way that we can disprove the hypothesis of currents of sensible extent within the magnet.
P9164 Besides this, we know that an electric current, in passing from
P9165 one part of a conductor to another, meets with resistance and gene rates heat ; so that if there were currents of the ordinary kind round portions of the magnet of sensible size, there would be a constant expenditure of energy required to maintain them, and a magnet
P9166 would be a perpetual source of heat. By confining the circuits to the molecules, within which nothing is known about resistance, we may assert, without fear of contradiction, that the current, in cir
P9167 culating within the molecule, meets with no resistance.
P9168 According to Ampere s theory, therefore, all the phenomena of magnetism are due to electric currents, and if we could make ob
P9169 servations of the magnetic force in the interior of a magnetic mole
P9170 cule, we should find that it obeyed exactly the same laws as the force in a region surrounded by any other electric circuit.
P9171 834.] In treating of the force in the interior of magnets, we have supposed the measurements to be made in a small crevasse hollowed
P9172 out of the substance of the magnet, Art. 395. We were thus led
P9173 to consider two different quantities, the magnetic force and the magnetic induction, both of which are supposed to be observed in
P9174 a space from which the magnetic matter is removed. We were
P9175 not supposed to be able to penetrate into the interior of a mag
P9176 netic molecule and to observe the force within it.
P9177 If we adopt Ampere s theory, we consider a magnet, not as a

P9363 851.] The first of these expressions, (18), was discovered by Gauss * in July 1835, and interpreted by him as a fundamental law of electrical action, that Two elements of electricity in a state of relative motion attract or repel one another, but not in the same way as if they are in a state of relative rest. This discovery was not, so far as I know, published in the lifetime of Gauss, so that the second expression, which was discovered independently by W.Weber, and published in the first part of his celebrated Elektrodynamische Maasbe&timmungen^, was the first result of the kind made known
P9364 to the scientific world.
P9365 852.] The two expressions lead to precisely the same result when
P9366 they are applied to the determination of the mechanical force be tween two electric currents, and this result is identical with that
P9367 of Ampere. But when they are considered as expressions of the physical law of the action between two electrical particles, we are led to enquire whether they are consistent with other known facts
P9368 Both of these expressions involve the relative velocity of the particles. Now, in establishing- by mathematical reasoning the well-known principle of the conservation of energy, it is generally assumed that the force acting between two particles is a function of the distance only, and it is commonly stated that if it is a function of anything else, such as the time, or the velocity of the particles, the proof would not hold.
P9369 Hence a law of electrical action, involving the velocity of the particles, has sometimes been supposed to be inconsistent with the
P9370 principle of the conservation of energy.
P9371 853.] The formula of Gauss is inconsistent with this principle, and must therefore be abandoned, as it leads to the conclusion that
P9372 energy might be indefinitely generated in a finite system by physical means. This objection does not apply to the formula of Weber, for he has shewn J that if we assume as the potential energy of a system consisting of two electric particles,
P9373 the repulsion between them, which is found by differentiating this quantity with respect to r, and changing the sign, is that given by the formula (19).

P9497 On Weber s Formula^ considered as resulting from an Action transmitted from one Electric Particle to the other with a Constant Velocity.
P9498 861.] In a very interesting letter of Gauss to W. Weber * he
P9499 refers to the electrodynamic speculations with which he had been occupied long before, and which he would have published if he could then have established that which he considered the real keystone of electrodynamics, namely, the deduction of the force acting be tween electric particles in motion from the consideration of an action between them, not instantaneous, but propagated in time, in a
P9500 similar manner to that of light. He had not succeeded in making
P9501 this deduction when he gave up his electrodynamic researches, and he had a subjective conviction that it would be necessary in the first place to form a consistent representation of the manner in
P9502 which the propagation takes place. Three eminent mathematicians have endeavoured to supply this
P9503 keystone of electrodynamics.
P9504 862. J In a memoir presented to the Royal Society of Gottingen in 1858, but afterwards withdrawn, and only published in Poggen dorff s Annalen in 1867, after the death of the author, Bernhard Riemann deduces the phenomena of the induction of electric cur rents from a modified form of Poisson s equation
P9505 where Fis the electrostatic potential, and a a velocity. This equation is of the same form as those which express the
P9506 propagation of waves and other disturbances in elastic media. The author, however, seems to avoid making explicit mention of any medium through which the propagation takes place.
P9507 The mathematical investigation given by Riemann has been ex amined by Clausiusf, who does not admit the soundness of the mathematical processes, and shews that the hypothesis that potential is propagated like light does not lead either to the formula of Weber, or to the known laws of electrodynamics.
P9508 863.] Clausius has also examined a far more elaborate investiga
P9509 tion by C. Neumann on the Principles of Electrodynamics J. Neu
P9510 mann, however, lias pointed out that his theory of the transmission of potential from one electric particle to another is quite different from that proposed by Gauss, adopted by Riemann, and criticized
P9511 * March 19, 1845, WerJse, bd. v. 629. Tubingen, 1868. t Pogg., bd. cxxxv. 612. Mathematische Annalen, i. 317. 436 ACTION AT A DISTANCE. [864.
P9512 by Clausius, in which the propagation is like that of light. There is, on the contrary, the greatest possible difference between the transmission of potential, according to Neumann, and the propaga
P9513 A luminous body sends forth light in all directions, the intensity
P9514 of which depends on the luminous body alone, and not on the
P9515 presence of the body which is enlightened by it.
P9516 An electric particle, on the other hand, sends forth a potential,
P9517 ed , depends not only on &amp;lt;?, the emitting particle,
P9518 but on e , the receiving particle, and on the distance r between the
P9519 particles at the instant of emission. In the case of light the intensity diminishes as the light is pro
P9520 pagated further from the luminous body ; the emitted potential flows to the body on which it acts without the slightest alteration
P9521 of its original value.
P9522 The light received by the illuminated body is in general only a fraction of that which falls on it ; the potential as received by the attracted body is identical with, or equal to, the potential which
P9523 Besides this, the velocity of transmission of the potential is not, like that of light, constant relative to the aether or to space, but rather like that of a projectile, constant relative to the velocity of the emitting particle at the instant of emission.
P9524 It appears, therefore, that in order to understand the theory of
P9525 Neumann, we must form a very different representation of the pro cess of the transmission of potential from that to which we have been accustomed in considering the propagation of light. Whether
P9526 it can ever be accepted as the construirbar Vorstellung of the process of transmission, which appeared necessary to Gauss, I cannot say, but I have not myself been able to construct a consistent
P9527 mental representation of Neumann s theory.
P9528 864.] Professor Betti*, of Pisa, has treated the subject in a
P9529 different way. He supposes the closed circuits in which the electric
P9530 currents flow to consist of elements each of which is polarized periodically, that is, at equidistant intervals of time. These polar ized elements act on one another as if they were little magnets whose axes are in the direction of the tangent to the circuits. The periodic time of this polarization is the same in all electric cir cuits. Betti supposes the action of one polarized element on an
P9531 * Nuovo Cimento, xxvii (1868). 866.] A MEDIUM NECESSARY. 437
P9532 other at a distance to take place, not instantaneously, but after a time proportional to the distance between the elements. In this
P9533 way he obtains expressions for the action of one electric circuit on another, which coincide with those which are known to be true. Clausius, however, has, in this case also, criticized some parts of the mathematical calculations into which we shall not here enter.
P9534 865.] There appears to be, in the minds of these eminent men, some prejudice, or a priori objection, against the hypothesis of a medium in which the phenomena of radiation of light and heat, and the electric actions at a distance take place. It is true that at one time those who speculated as to the causes of physical pheno mena, were in the habit of accounting for each kind of action at a distance by means of a special sethereal fluid, whose function and property it was to produce these actions. They filled all space three and four times over with aethers of different kinds, the pro perties of which were invented merely to save appearances, so that
P9535 more rational enquirers were willing rather to accept not only New
P9536 ton s definite law of attraction at a distance, but even the dogma of
P9537 Cotes &quot;*, that action at a distance is one of the primary properties of matter, and that no explanation can be more intelligible than this
P9538 fact. Hence the undulatory theory of light has met with much
P9539 opposition, directed not against its failure to explain the pheno mena, but against its assumption of the existence of a medium in which light is propagated.
P9540 866.] We have seen that the mathematical expressions for electro
P9541 dynamic action led, in the mind of Gauss, to the conviction that a theory of the propagation of electric action in time would be found
P9542 to be the very key-stone of electrodynamics. Now we are unable
P9543 to conceive of propagation in time, except either as the flight of a material substance through space, or as the propagation of a con
P9544 dition of motion or stress in a medium already existing in space. In the theory of Neumann, the mathematical conception called Potential, which we are unable to conceive as a material substance,
P9545 is supposed to be projected from one particle to another, in a manner
P9546 which is quite independent of a medium, and which, as Neumann
P9547 has himself pointed out, is extremely different from that of the pro pagation of light. In the theories of Riemann and Betti it would appear that the action is supposed to be propagated in a manner somewhat more similar to that of light.
P9548 But in all of these theories the question naturally occurs : If
P9549 * Preface to Newton s Principia, 2nd edition. 438 ACTION AT A DISTANCE. [866.
P9550 something is transmitted from one particle to another at a distance, what is its condition after it has left the one particle and before
P9551 it has reached the other ? If this something is the potential energy
P9552 of the two particles, as in Neumann s theory, how are we to con
P9553 ceive this energy as existing in a point of space, coinciding neither with the one particle nor with the other ? In fact, whenever energy is transmitted from one body to another in time, there must be
P9554 a medium or substance in which the energy exists after it leaves one body and before it reaches the other, for energy, as Torricelli *
P9555 remarked, is a quintessence of so subtile a nature that it cannot be contained in any vessel except the inmost substance of material
P9556 things. Hence all these theories lead to the conception of a medium in which the propagation takes place, and if we admit this medium as an hypothesis, I think it ought to occupy a prominent place in our investigations, and that we ought to endeavour to construct a mental representation of all the details of its action, and this has
P9557 been my constant aim in this treatise.
